❯ The_Relay_Assignment_Brief.pdf                                                

⏺ I read the brief. The core is a 5-step RAG pipeline (understand, retrieve,
  decide, draft, check) over 3 arXiv papers on Groq's free tier. It also needs a
  controllable fake model server, load tests, a results.csv, and a 2-page
  write-up. They grade how you direct me and where you overrule me, so the stack
  choice should be yours.

  Stack options
  - A. Python asyncio (my recommendation):
    - Pipeline on httpx with streaming, so first-token time is real.
    - Stand-in server on Starlette/uvicorn, streaming the reply in pieces, with
      knobs for slowness, failures, rate-limit responses and getting slower
      under load.
    - PDFs parsed with pymupdf, keeping page and section for citations. pandas
      computes the tables from results.csv.
    - Managed with uv. It's the fastest to build and the easiest to defend in
      the live session.
  - B. Go throughout:
    - Goroutines and channels make the queueing, admission control and load
      generation feel natural, and timing jitter is lower.
    - Downsides: PDF section extraction is weak in Go, there are no good BM25 or
      embedding libraries, and there's more boilerplate. That probably costs 2
      to 3 hours of the 6 to 8 hour budget.
  - C. Python pipeline, Go stand-in:
    - The stand-in can take heavy concurrency without the load generator and the
      server competing for one event loop.
    - It's a good story, but two codebases is more than this assignment needs.

  Retrieval options (Table 3 needs 3 settings anyway)
  - BM25 only: costs no tokens and no network calls. It's weak on paraphrased
    questions.
  - Local embeddings (fastembed with bge-small on CPU) plus BM25 hybrid: better
    recall, still free.
  - Re-ranking: either a local cross-encoder, or an LLM re-rank that spends Groq
    tokens. The LLM version makes a good "drop it when the limit is close"
    lever.

  Design decision to make early: use a small Groq model (for example
  llama-3.1-8b-instant) for the understand and check steps, and a larger one for
  drafting. Their per-model limits differ, and that split probably shapes
  everything else.

  Before we start
  - No GROQ_API_KEY is set in your environment. Create a free key at
    console.groq.com and put it in .env. I won't read or commit it.
  - gh is logged in as yashdagar, so the repo will go under that account with
    your personal commit identity.

  Done so far
  - Created ~/Documents/Github/the-relay with git set to your personal identity,
    Python 3.12 through uv, and the dependencies installed.
  - Downloaded the three papers at the exact versions into data/papers/, and
    checked they are valid PDFs (15, 66 and 39 pages).
  - src/relay/library.py splits the papers into pieces of a chosen size with 15%
    overlap, and tags each piece with its section and page from the PDF's table
    of contents, for example [1706.03762v7 §Scaled Dot-Product Attention p.4].
  - Piece counts at sizes 120, 300 and 600 words are 608, 273 and 166. I checked
    that section tagging is right for the Attention and Dynamic Chunking
    papers.

  Things to use in the "mislead a naive retriever" section
  - Moshi dominates: it is about half of all pieces, so any query using general
    terms gets pulled toward it.
  - Repeated section names: Moshi has two sections called "Architecture", one
    for the text model and one for the audio codec, and all three papers have
    attention or related-work sections.
  - "Chunking" means two things: the paper's own topic, and how we split the
    papers.
  - Reference lists match keywords: they score well on author and title words.
    In the Attention paper, pages 13 to 15 (the attention visualizations) also
    get tagged as References.

  Proposed next steps (tell me what to change)
  1. Real-service probe: a small, budgeted script against Groq that records time
     to first token and total time with streaming, plus the rate-limit headers
     (x-ratelimit-*, retry-after) and what a 429 response looks like. This is
     where Table 1 comes from.
  2. Pipeline:
     - Steps: classify the question (small model), retrieve (BM25 or hybrid, no
       model call), decide (answer, ask a narrower question, or refuse when
       retrieval scores are low), draft with citations (larger model, streamed),
       check that citations exist and the reply is backed by the passages
       (small model). Loop back to retrieve once if the check fails.
     - Every request passes a token-bucket gate that follows the limit headers.
  3. Stand-in server: Starlette, OpenAI-compatible streaming, with settings for
     latency spread, failure rate, per-minute and per-day limits, slowdown as
     more requests run at once, and slow successes. Calibrated from step 1.
  4. Load generator and results.csv writer, then analysis that computes every
     table from the CSV.

  Needed from you
  - Groq key: create a free key at console.groq.com and put it in .env as
    GROQ_API_KEY. The probe can't run until it's there.
  - Push approval: say when you want me to create the public yashdagar/the-relay
    repo and push this first commit.
  - Conversation history: the brief wants your real conversation with me,
    unedited. Keep this session (/export works), and make the main design calls
    yourself, like the model split and refusal threshold above, so the history
    shows you directing the work.

⏺ The architecture is written up in the README with two diagrams, the repo is
  cleaned up, and the conventions are in place. Everything is staged but not
  committed or pushed.

  Architecture (README.md)
  - Components diagram: load generator, admission queue, pipeline, budget
    governor, model client, retrieval tool and recorder. The model client points
    at either Groq or the stand-in by changing only the base URL.
  - Request flow diagram: the five steps. The decide step can refuse, retrieve
    again (at most 2 extra rounds) or go on to draft. The check step can redraft
    once, then refuses.
  - Degradation ladder: full, then lean, then extractive (send matching passages
    verbatim with citations), then an honest "budget is gone" reply. Sending a
    wrong answer is never a step down the ladder.
  - Repository layout: the modules we plan to write. I didn't create empty
    placeholder files for them.
  - I rendered both diagrams with mermaid-cli and looked at them; they come out
    as intended.

  Cleanup
  - Removed the uv scaffold leftovers: the hello-world main, the unused relay
    command entry, the placeholder description and the cache files.
  - Added ruff (line length 100) and pytest as dev dependencies. Lint and format
    checks pass.

  Conventions
  - CONTRIBUTING.md:
    - Branches are named <area>/<desc>, and commit subjects start with <area>:
      followed by an imperative summary.
    - Commit bodies say what goal the change trades away.
    - Each results run gets its own directory under results/, and runs are never
      edited, since reviewers recompute the tables from them.
    - All config values live in one module, with the unit in the name.
    - Test only through public interfaces, and use the stand-in rather than
      hand-written mocks.
  - .github/pull_request_template.md has What, Why, Trade-off, Evidence (before
    and after numbers from a run), and a checklist.
  - .gitmessage is set as this clone's commit template.

  A .env file has appeared in the repo. It's git-ignored and not staged, and I
  haven't read it.

  One design call before the pipeline code
  The brief says the reply is checked before it's sent, and that conflicts with
  time to first token. There are three options:
  - Hold the draft until the check passes, then stream it (my lean). This never
    sends something wrong, but first token arrives roughly when the draft
    finishes.
  - Stream the draft as it's written and take it back if the check fails. First
    token comes fast, but the person may already have read something
    embarrassing.
  - Stream an immediate cited-passages preview, then the checked answer. First
    token comes fast and nothing unchecked is sent, but it takes more work and
    makes the definition of "first token" debatable.

  Which one you pick shapes what "fast" means in the write-up, so it should be
  your call. Tell me, and whether to make the first commit and create the public
  yashdagar/the-relay repo now.

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 8 lines
      67  
      68  The branch and loop the brief asks for are the decide step (ref
          use, retrieve again, or draft) and the check step (redraft once
          , then refuse).
      69  
      70 +### The decision that shapes everything: hold the draft until i
         +t passes the check                                             
      71 +                                                               
      72 +Nothing reaches the person until the draft has passed the check
         +. The draft is generated in full and buffered, checked for grou
         +nded citations, and only then streamed out. "First token" in ev
         +ery table means the first token the person sees, which is after
         + the check.                                                    
      73 +                                                               
      74 +- **Gives up:** time to first token. It becomes roughly draft t
         +ime plus check time instead of the model's own first-token late
         +ncy.                                                           
      75 +- **Gets:** no unchecked text is ever shown, so there is nothin
         +g to retract. A wrong or embarrassing answer costs more than a 
         +slow one.                                                      
      76 +- **Consequence for load:** every request holds its slot for th
         +e full draft and check, so concurrency limits and deadlines are
         + set on total time, not first-token time.                      
      77 +                                                               
      78  ### Degradation ladder
      79  
      80  When the governor says a step cannot be afforded in time, the p
          ipeline steps down instead of queueing until the person gives u
          p:

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 17 lines
      105  tests/
      106  ```
      107  
      108 +## Tests                                                      
      109 +                                                              
      110 +The tests were written before the code and define each layer's
          + contract. Thresholds live in `tests/thresholds.py`; the gold 
          +questions in `data/questions/eval.jsonl`.                     
      111 +                                                              
      112 +- **Gold set** (`tests/test_gold_questions.py`): 36 questions 
          +(25 answerable, 5 the papers do not answer, 3 out of scope, 3 
          +prompt injections). Every evidence phrase must exist verbatim 
          +in its paper and fit inside one piece at every piece size.    
      113 +- **Metrics** (`tests/unit/`): precision@k, recall@k, MRR, per
          +centiles, groundedness, answer grading and every write-up tabl
          +e, checked on hand-built inputs.                              
      114 +- **Retrieval** (`tests/retrieval/`): recall@k, MRR and precis
          +ion@k of the default setting on the gold set, search latency, 
          +and behaviour such as never returning reference lists.        
      115 +- **Generation** (`tests/generation/`, `-m real`): groundednes
          +s of every sent answer, correct rate, wrong rate, refusal of u
          +nanswerable questions, over-refusal.                          
      116 +- **Application** (`tests/application/`): out-of-scope and inj
          +ection handling against Groq (`-m real`), and pipeline guarant
          +ees against the stand-in: unchecked drafts are never sent, fir
          +st token only after the check, bounded loops, retries, deadlin
          +es, daily budget exhaustion.                                  
      117 +- **System** (`tests/system/`, `slow`): every load shape again
          +st the stand-in, and the submitted `results/results.csv` again
          +st the brief's schema.                                        
      118 +                                                              
      119 +```sh                                                         
      120 +uv run pytest                 # everything except Groq calls  
      121 +uv run pytest -m real         # spends the Groq daily budget  
      122 +RELAY_EVAL_REPLIES=results/<run>/replies.jsonl uv run pytest -
          +m real   # regrade saved replies for free

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The tests for every layer are written, but none of them can pass yet: all 8
  test files fail at import because the modules they test (relay.eval,
  relay.tables, relay.config and the rest) don't exist yet. Nothing is
  committed.

  Gold set (data/questions/eval.jsonl, 36 questions)
  - Mix: 25 answerable questions built from facts in the papers, 5 the papers
    don't answer, 3 off-topic, and 3 that try to override the assistant's
    instructions (prompt injection).
  - Unanswerable ones are checked: for each, I searched the paper text, and the
    papers don't mention it (H-Net never names GPUs, dropout or a license).
  - Evidence is checked: each answerable question carries the exact phrases from
    the paper that answer it, so relevance doesn't depend on piece size. A
    throwaway script confirmed all 36 phrases appear in the right paper and fit
    inside one piece at sizes 120, 300 and 600 words.
  - Traps built in:
    - The Attention paper uses two beam settings: size 4 for translation, size
      21 for parsing.
    - The H-Net paper's abstract says nearly 4× better data efficiency on DNA,
      and the body says 3.6×.
    - One question needs both the Attention and the H-Net papers.

  Tests by layer
  - Retrieval (tests/retrieval/test_retrieval.py): precision@k, recall@k and MRR
    for the default setting, plus search latency. Also: results are
    deterministic, can be limited to one paper, and never return reference
    lists.
  - Generation (tests/generation/test_generation.py, spends Groq budget):
    - Groundedness is checked mechanically: every number in an answer must
      appear in the passages it cites, and it may only cite passages that were
      retrieved.
    - Relevance: the required facts must be present and the cited paper must be
      the right one.
    - Refusal: unanswerable questions are refused, and answerable ones are
      rarely refused.
  - Application:
    - Against Groq (test_out_of_scope_and_injection.py): off-topic questions are
      refused, and injected instructions aren't followed.
    - Against the stand-in, so free (test_pipeline_guarantees.py):
      - An uncited draft, or one citing a passage that wasn't retrieved, is
        never sent.
      - First token only after the check passes.
      - One redraft, then refuse; model calls per request are capped.
      - Retries on rate limits, giving up at the deadline.
      - Daily budget exhausted: fast reply that says when it resets.
  - Metrics from the brief:
    - tests/unit/test_tables.py checks every column of Tables 2a, 2b and 3
      against hand-computed numbers.
    - tests/system/test_load_shapes.py runs the five required load shapes
      against the stand-in.
    - tests/system/test_results_file.py checks results.csv against the brief's
      schema.
  - README.md now records your hold-until-checked decision, what it costs in
    time to first token, and how to run each test layer.
  - Groq replies are saved per run, and RELAY_EVAL_REPLIES regrades a saved run
    without spending budget again.

  Choices I made inside the tests that you should confirm or overrule
  - Thresholds (tests/thresholds.py) are my provisional numbers:
    - Recall@k ≥ 0.80, MRR ≥ 0.60, precision@k ≥ 0.20. Precision is low because
      most questions have only one relevant passage.
    - 100% of sent answers grounded, ≥80% correct, ≤5% wrong.
    - 100% of unanswerable questions refused.
    - Steady load succeeds ≥99%; ≤10% of requests given up during a burst.
    - The load numbers should be revisited after measuring Groq.
  - Every request gets a grade, failures included. A timeout on an answerable
    question counts as refused_wrongly, so failing under load shows up as lost
    quality.
  - Reference lists are excluded from retrieval.
  - When the daily budget is gone, the reply may be a degraded one (verbatim
    passages with citations) or a plain refusal. Either is allowed, but it must
    never be an unchecked answer.
  - I added a queue_depth column to results.csv. Table 2b needs peak queue
    length, and the brief's columns can't produce it.

  Next is writing relay.eval and relay.tables until the gold-set and metric
  tests pass, then retrieval. Should I commit this as the first commit and
  create the public yashdagar/the-relay repo now?

⏺ relay.eval is written and its tests pass: 29 of 29 for the metric tests and
  the gold-set checks. It's on the eval/metrics-and-grading branch and not
  committed yet.

  Repo
  - https://github.com/yashdagar/the-relay is public and holds three commits on
    main: scaffold and conventions, the paper splitter, and the tests plus gold
    questions.
  - Commits use your personal identity, and .env is not tracked.

  src/relay/eval.py
  - Evidence matching: text is lowercased, words split by a line-break hyphen
    are rejoined, and the phrase must match on whole words, so 4000 does not
    match 40000.
  - Retrieval metrics:
    - precision@k divides by k, so returning fewer pieces doesn't inflate it.
    - Recall raises an error when a question has no evidence, rather than
      quietly counting as 0 or 1.
    - Percentiles use numpy's linear method, which pandas also uses, so
      reviewers recomputing from results.csv in pandas get the same numbers.
  - is_grounded: an answer must cite at least one passage and only passages that
    were retrieved. Every number in it, ignoring the citation markers, must
    appear in the passages it cites.
  - grade: produces the four grades from the brief. A degraded extractive reply
    counts as a refusal, so serving raw passages during an outage never counts
    as a correct answer.
  - Reply is defined in src/relay/pipeline.py, because the tests import it from
    there. The pipeline itself will go in that same file.

  Fix to the gold set
  - The evidence-phrase test caught a bug in my question data: the evidence
    phrase for mos-08 could never match, because in the PDF a footnote marker is
    glued to the name ("WavLM8").
  - My earlier throwaway check missed it because it matched plain substrings. I
    swapped in a clean phrase from the same paragraph: "WavLM projects a 16kHz
    waveform into 1024-dimensional embeddings".

  Should I commit this on the branch? Next is relay.tables, so
  tests/unit/test_tables.py passes, then retrieval.

⏺ relay.tables is written, and all 33 unit tests now pass: the metric tests plus
  the 13 table tests. It's not committed yet. The eval work and the mos-08
  evidence fix are committed as two commits on eval/metrics-and-grading.

  src/relay/tables.py
  - RESULT_COLUMNS: the brief's 12 columns in order, plus queue_depth.
  - latency_table: one row per scenario and source, so a real and a stand-in run
    of the same scenario stay separate.
    - Requests sent.
    - Duration, from the first request's start to the last request's finish.
    - Offered rate: requests per minute over the window when requests arrived.
      If every request arrives at the same instant, it falls back to the
      duration.
    - First-token p50/p95/p99, counting only requests that got a first token.
    - Total-time p50/p95/p99 over every request, failures included.
    - A percentile with nothing to measure comes out empty (NaN), which the
      write-up shows as "not measured".
  - outcomes_table:
    - Success, rate-limited, timed-out and error percentages to one decimal
      place. I added error % because the brief's table has no column for it.
    - Retries per request.
    - Peak queue length.
    - Tokens per answer, counting successful requests only.
    - Cost per 1,000 answers from token prices you pass in. The Groq free tier
      costs $0, so the write-up should use Groq's paid list price and say so.
  - retrieval_table: one row per retrieval setting for the retrieval runs.
    - Correct, wrong, correctly-refused and wrongly-refused percentages.
    - Tokens per answer.
    - First-token and total-time p50/p95.

  Full suite: 42 passed, 9 skipped, 30 errors. The skips are the results.csv
  checks, since no runs exist yet. The errors come from tests that need modules
  not written yet: relay.retrieval, relay.config, relay.standin, relay.load.

  Should I commit this on tables/write-up-tables and move on to retrieval?

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Retrieval is written and its 12 tests pass with the current default,
  300w-k5-hybrid. The full set of unit and gold-set tests passes too. Nothing is
  committed yet on retrieval/bm25-hybrid.

  src/relay/retrieval.py
  - BM25: keyword matching with a small stopword list.
  - Hybrid: BM25 plus bge-small embeddings, merged by rank (reciprocal rank
    fusion). The embeddings run on CPU and are cached per piece size.
  - Rerank: an optional MiniLM cross-encoder re-scores the top 20.
  - Exclusions and filters: reference lists are never returned, and a search can
    be limited to named papers.
  - Models download to .cache/, which is git-ignored.

  Measured on the 27 questions that expect an answer
  - 120w-k8-bm25: recall 0.889, MRR 0.614, precision 0.134, p95 1.6 ms.
  - 300w-k5-bm25: recall 0.944, MRR 0.806, precision 0.222, p95 0.8 ms.
  - 300w-k5-hybrid: recall 0.907, MRR 0.864, precision 0.215, p95 9.6 ms.
  - 300w-k5-hybrid-rerank: recall 0.889, MRR 0.762, precision 0.215, p95 1489
    ms. It is worse and about 150 times slower. The reranker was trained on
    short web passages, and our 300-word scientific pieces get truncated.
  - 600w-k3-hybrid: recall 0.852, MRR 0.846, precision 0.296, p95 10.6 ms.

  Label correction (you should know about this one)
  - After the first measurement, I found the gold evidence for mos-02 and mos-06
    was incomplete.
  - The p.24 table also gives Mimi's 12.5 Hz and 1.1 kbps, and the Helium facts
    also appear on p.6 and p.21.
  - I changed the evidence format so each fact can list alternative phrases,
    with 2 new unit tests. I added those alternatives to the two questions.
  - This raised hybrid's recall from 0.870 to 0.907. It's a change made after
    seeing results, so the commit message will say so.

  Misses that are real
  - mos-06: the only piece that has both facts together is the intro bullet
    list, and no setting ranks it in the top 5.
  - x-01 is half found, because one search can't cover two papers well. That's
    what the decide step's extra retrieval round is for.
  - dc-07 is missed by hybrid only.

  Default setting: your call
  BM25 at 300 words has the best measured recall, costs nothing and takes under
  1 ms. Hybrid ranks the right piece higher and should cope better with
  paraphrased questions. But my gold questions were written from the paper text,
  which favours keyword matching, so that advantage is untested. Table 3 needs
  answer quality per setting from real Groq runs anyway.

  I recommend making 300w-k5-bm25 the default now, since it follows the
  measurements. Then add about 5 paraphrased questions to test hybrid's claimed
  advantage before the Table 3 runs. Should I switch the default, or keep
  hybrid?

✻ Baked for 11m 57s · done 12:46 AM

※ recap: We're building The Relay, a cited Q&A pipeline over three arXiv papers, and retrieval is now working and passing its tests (uncommitted). Next, you choose the default retrieval setting: BM25 at 300 words, which I recommend, or hybrid.

❯ what are the p99 scores                                                       

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ These are p99 search latencies. I ran all 36 questions 5 times per setting,
  180 searches each, after the models had loaded:

  - 120w-k8-bm25: p50 0.9, p95 3.4, p99 6.8, max 12.4 ms.
  - 300w-k5-bm25: p50 0.6, p95 1.3, p99 3.3, max 6.7 ms.
  - 300w-k5-hybrid: p50 6.5, p95 29.7, p99 43.0, max 115.0 ms.
  - 300w-k5-hybrid-rerank: p50 1547, p95 1632, p99 3650, max 5608 ms.
  - 600w-k3-hybrid: p50 7.2, p95 11.7, p99 13.5, max 14.7 ms.
  - Hybrid's tail is unstable. In the earlier run, 300w-k5-hybrid had p95 9.6
    ms; here it's 29.7 ms with a 115 ms max. The query embedding runs on CPU and
    is sensitive to whatever else the machine is doing. BM25 stays under 7 ms
    even at the max.
  - Rerank's p99 is more than twice its p50. At 3.6 s it alone would eat a large
    part of a request's deadline.
  - None of this matters much yet. A Groq model call is likely hundreds of
    milliseconds or more (we'll measure it), so retrieval is noise next to the
    model calls, except for rerank.
  - This settles one thing: rerank is out for the main path. It is slower and
    less accurate, so it stays only as a compared row in Table 3.

  These are throwaway measurements; the script is at /private/tmp/claude-501/-Us
  ers-yash/279ac597-1f39-4ea2-b20e-1a573fedfea2/scratchpad/retrieval_p99.py. If
  you want them in the write-up, I'll record them as a run under results/.

  The default-setting question is still open: BM25 at 300 words (my
  recommendation), or keep hybrid?

⏺ I ran the first check against Groq: 4 requests, one per chat model this key
  can use. The results are committed on client/streaming-probe, along with the
  streaming client and a probe command.

  What the responses showed (details in
  results/20261111-0148-probe-limits/NOTES.md)
  - No Llama models on this key. The candidates are gpt-oss-20b, gpt-oss-120b,
    qwen3.8-27b and allam-2-7b.
  - Limits are per model. The three larger models each allow 1,000 requests per
    day and 8,000 tokens per minute. allam-2-7b allows 7,000 per day and 6,000
    per minute.
  - The per-minute token limit is charged on what you might use, not what you 
    used. Each call cost its prompt tokens plus its max_tokens setting, not the
    tokens it actually generated. For example, a 78-token prompt with max_tokens
    64 cost 142, though only 31 tokens came back. All four models followed this
    rule. So setting max_tokens loosely costs throughput directly.
  - The daily request budget comes back gradually. It refills one request every
    86.4 seconds (86,400 s ÷ 1,000), not all at midnight. That directly shapes
    the "3pm, budget gone" section: the reply can say roughly when capacity
    returns.
  - Not visible in any header: requests per minute and tokens per day. The docs
    say 30 per minute and 200K tokens per day for the GPT-OSS models. I'll treat
    those as low confidence until a probe hits them.
  - GPT-OSS models stream hidden reasoning first. Their first visible token came
    at about 500 ms. qwen and allam answered in about 136 ms.

  What this means for the design
  - Per-minute tokens decide throughput. One draft call is about 2,000 context
    tokens, plus the prompt, plus a 400-token max_tokens. That's roughly 2,700
    tokens, so one model fits about 3 drafts a minute.
  - Tokens per day probably decide the daily budget. If 200K per day is right,
    one draft model handles about 70 answers a day. The Table 3 runs alone (36
    questions × 5 settings) would exceed that on a single model.
  - Spreading steps across models multiplies capacity. Because limits are per
    model, each model used adds its own allowance.

  Proposed probe plan (about 120 requests, spread across models)
  - Latency: about 20 streamed calls each on qwen, gpt-oss-20b and gpt-oss-120b,
    using a realistic draft prompt, paced under the per-minute token limit.
  - Requests-per-minute limit: a burst of about 40 tiny calls on allam, which
    has the biggest daily allowance. This captures the 429 response body,
    retry-after, recovery time, and how many requests can run at once.
  - Per-minute token limit: about 4 back-to-back large calls on one model, to
    see what its 429 looks like.
  - Daily limits: not exhausted on purpose, since running out would block the
    rest of today's work. If it happens naturally later, we'll record it.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which Groq model should each pipeline step use? Limits are per model (1K 
     requests/day, 8K tokens/min each), so spreading steps across models 
     multiplies capacity. → Decide after latency probe
     · Run the proposed real-service probe plan (~120 requests: latency x3 
     models, RPM burst on allam, TPM burst, no deliberate daily exhaustion)? → 
     Run it as proposed (Recommended)

⏺ Running the plan as proposed, and choosing models after the latency numbers.
  Adding the three probe phases.

      120 +    for seq in range(TPM_BURST_SIZE):                         
      121 +        result = await client.chat(                           
      122 +            TPM_BURST_MODEL,                                  
      123 +            messages,                                         
      124 +            max_tokens=DRAFT_MAX_TOKENS,                      
      125 +            deadline_s=60,                                    
      126 +            step="probe-tpm",                                 
      127 +            extra=MODEL_EXTRA.get(TPM_BURST_MODEL),           
      128 +        )                                                     
      129 +        rows.append(_tag(result, seq=seq))                    
      130 +    return rows                                               
      131 +                                                              
      132 +                                                              
      133 +PHASES = {"limits": limits, "latency": latency, "rpm": rpm, "t
          +pm": tpm}                                                     
      134 +                                                              
      135 +                                                              
      136  async def _run(phase: str) -> None:
      137      load_dotenv(ROOT / ".env")
      138      client = ModelClient(GROQ_BASE_URL, os.environ["GROQ_API_K
           EY"])

      7 +- "Requested 2204" is less than prompt plus max_tokens (2329), s

        +it is close enough to plan with prompt + max_tokens.

        + not once per minute.                                           

⏺ I finished the probe plan with 109 Groq requests, without hitting a daily
  limit. The client, probe code and four results runs are on
  client/streaming-probe and pushed.

  Rejections
  - Requests per minute: I fired 40 calls at once against a limit of 30. 33
    succeeded and 7 got a 429 within about 230 ms, with retry-after: 2. They
    were refused fast, not slowly. Recovery came within 5.1 s. No limit on
    requests in flight showed up at 40 at once.
  - Tokens per minute: the 4th of 4 back-to-back draft calls was refused in 91
    ms.
  - Grader bugs found: the first grading of real answers exposed 5 bugs in my
    grader. I fixed them with 9 new tests. The fixed grader still flags a real
    mistake: gpt-oss-120b added "Chen et al., 2022" from memory.

  Numbers you couldn't guess without running it
  - Tokens-per-minute 429: retry-after says 1 s, but the error body says 82.5
    ms. The header rounds up to whole seconds, so a client that trusts only the
    header waits about 12 times longer than it needs to. The retry policy should
    read the body.
  - Per-minute budget is charged up front: a call costs its prompt plus
    max_tokens. With a 400-token cap and about 120 tokens actually used, roughly
    70% of the reserved output budget goes unused.
  - Both limits refill continuously: the daily one refills one request per 86.4
    s, and the per-minute one about 133 tokens per second.

  Candidate draft models (20 real draft calls each)
  - qwen/qwen3.8-27b:
    - 17 of 20 correct.
    - First token p50 329 ms, p95 486 ms.
    - Total p50 482 ms, p95 1301 ms.
    - Downsides: it's a "preview" model, which Groq says not to use in
      production. It once said "The papers do not say" and then quoted the
      answer.
  - gpt-oss-120b:
    - 16 of 20 correct.
    - First token p50 481 ms, p95 870 ms.
    - Total p50 697 ms, p95 1611 ms.
    - It's a production model; it added an outside citation once.
  - gpt-oss-20b:
    - 16 of 20 correct.
    - First token p50 614 ms, p95 1877 ms.
    - Total p50 680 ms, p95 1925 ms.
    - It has the worst tail.

  With 20 calls each, the quality gaps are within noise. Because we hold the
  reply until the check passes, total time matters more than first token.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Model per pipeline step? Each model has its own 1K req/day and 8K 
     tokens/min, and the check should ideally use a different model family than 
     the draft so their errors are independent. → 120b drafts, qwen checks 
     (Recommended)

  qwen3.8-27b checks. I'm saving the project context to memory, then building
  the config, stand-in, budget governor and pipeline.

  setting a score threshold of 7.0 to refuse the three clearly off-topic ones
  before any model call. The draft and check steps will handle the rest,
  starting with the config.

  Decision for when you're back: what limits the stand-in emulates for the load
  shapes. At free-tier limits the system can only take about 3 answers a minute,
  so a steady run of a few minutes yields about 10 requests, too few for a
  meaningful p99.

  - My proposal: emulate Groq's documented Developer-plan limits (250K tokens
    and 1K requests per minute) for the stand-in rows. Capacity is then roughly
    85 answers a minute, and the five shapes run at about 60/min steady, a
    600/min 20 s burst, 200/min sustained overload, halved limits, and a
    40-requests-per-day cap.
  - The real row: today's paced evaluation run doubles as the "steady, real" row
    in Table 2a, at 3 a minute.

  New finding from Groq's price list (https://console.groq.com/docs/models):

  - gpt-oss-120b (draft): $0.15 per million input tokens, $0.60 per million
    output.
  - gpt-oss-20b (understand): $0.075 in, $0.30 out.
  - qwen3.8-27b (check): $0.80 in, $4.00 out. That's 5× the draft model's price.

  So the check step, which reads all the passages again, is likely about 80% of
  the cost per answer. That directly conflicts with "the bill small", and you
  should decide it once the numbers are in. Since results.csv has no per-model
  token counts, I'll add a per-request cost column computed from each request's
  step trace, so cost per 1,000 answers is computable from the file.

         +258 ms to return 49 tokens on the routing call, against 537 ms 
         +usually.                                                       
      64  
      65  ## 5. When a limit is hit
      66  
      67 -**Per minute:** the governor holds the request until tokens ref
         -ill, but only if that still leaves 3.1 s for draft and check be
         -fore the 15 s deadline. Otherwise it drafts on the lean model w
         -ith three pieces (24 of 143 under overload, 17 of 71 with halve
         -d limits). Otherwise the person gets the top passages, quoted a
         -nd marked unchecked, with "try again in a minute". So everyone 
         -is delayed up to about 13 s, some get a shorter answer from a s
         -maller model, and nobody is dropped silently. In the burst, 20 
         -of 50 got passages instead of an answer, and none waited past 1
         -4.5 s. **Per day:** the budget is checked before any model call
         -. If the draft or check model has nothing left, the reply comes
         - back in about 6 ms. If only the router's model is out, routing
         - is skipped and the score filter does its job.                 
      67 +**Per minute:** the governor holds the request until tokens ref
         +ill, but only if that still leaves 3.1 s for draft and check be
         +fore the 15 s deadline. Otherwise it drafts on the lean model w
         +ith three pieces (24 lean drafts across 143 requests under over
         +load, 17 across 71 with halved limits). Otherwise the person ge
         +ts the top passages, quoted and marked unchecked, with "try aga
         +in in a minute". So everyone is delayed up to about 13 s, some 
         +get a shorter answer from a smaller model, and nobody is droppe
         +d silently. In the burst, 20 of 50 got passages instead of an a
         +nswer, and none took longer than 14.6 s. **Per day:** the budge
         +t is checked before any model call. If the draft or check model
         + has nothing left, the reply comes back in about 6 ms. If only 
         +the router's model is out, routing is skipped and the score fil
         +ter does its job.

      82  ## How this library will mislead a naive retriever
      83  
      84  1. **One paper talks about another.** "Transformer" appears all
           through the H-Net paper, so for x-01 (Transformer encoder laye
          rs versus H-Net's) a plain search fills every slot from H-Net. 
          Fix: paper-scoped sub-searches.
      85 -2. **The right words in the wrong section.** dc-07 asks what da
         -ta the H-Net models were trained on; the Chinese-and-code secti
         -on repeats the phrase "data budget" and wins. And un-03 (WMT 20
         -16 English-to-Romanian BLEU, not in the paper) scores 19.6, hig
         -her than 18 answerable questions.                              
      85 +2. **The right words in the wrong section.** dc-07 asks what da
         +ta the H-Net models were trained on; the Chinese-and-code secti
         +on repeats the phrase "data budget" and wins. And un-03 (WMT 20
         +16 English-to-Romanian BLEU, not in the paper) scores 19.6 on B
         +M25, higher than 14 of the 25 answerable questions.            
      86  3. **Facts split across pieces and pages.** At 120 words dc-01 
          loses the smoothing module and answers "routing module and down
          sampler", grounded and wrong. Moshi's Fisher size breaks across
           a page as "It consists" / "of 2000 hours".

       18 +A Python asyncio pipeline with five steps. \textbf{Understand}
          + (gpt-oss-20b) decides if the question is about the three pape
          +rs and splits two-part questions into paper-scoped searches. \
          +textbf{Retrieve} is a local BM25 tool over 300-word pieces tag
          +ged with paper, section and page. \textbf{Decide} refuses with
          +out any model call when the best BM25 score is under 7.0. \tex
          +tbf{Draft} (gpt-oss-120b) answers only from the passages and c
          +ites passage ids. \textbf{Check} runs two gates: a determinist
          +ic one (every cited id was retrieved, every number in the answ
          +er appears in its cited passage) and a model one (qwen3.8-27b,
          + a different family, confirms support). A failed check gets on
          +e redraft with specific feedback, then a refusal. A per-model 
          +budget governor mirrors Groq's buckets, every request has a 15
          + s deadline, and under pressure the pipeline steps down: lean 
          +draft on gpt-oss-20b with three pieces, then the best passages
          + quoted and labelled unchecked, then an honest budget message.
          + A wrong answer is never on that ladder.                      
       19 +                                                              
       20 +The decision that shaped everything: \textbf{nothing reaches t
          +he person until the check passes.} So in every table, first to
          +ken equals total time. I gave up time to first token (about 0.
          +5 s from the model, about 2 s after the check) to never retrac
          +t a sentence. Because a request holds its slot through draft a
          +nd check, all my deadlines are on total time.

       37 +Two things I only learned by running it. Tokens per minute are
          + charged as prompt plus \texttt{max\_tokens} at admission, not
          + tokens generated (78 in + 31 out cost 142 = 78 + 64). And \te
          +xttt{retry-after} rounds up to whole seconds: it said 1 s when
          + the body said 82.5 ms.                                       
       38 +                                                              
       39 +\textbf{The stand-in} is a Starlette server speaking the same 
          +streaming API, so switching is a base URL change. Each model h
          +as per-minute and per-day buckets that refill continuously, ch
          +arge prompt plus \texttt{max\_tokens}, and refuse with Groq-sh
          +aped 429s. Time to first token is log-normal per model, fitted
          + to the p50 and p95 of 20 real draft calls each (gpt-oss-120b 
          +481 ms, sigma 0.36). It can fail, succeed slowly, slow down ab
          +ove 16 calls in flight, and stream a few words at a time. For 
          +gold questions it answers from the gold evidence, but only if 
          +retrieval sent that passage. \textbf{Where it differs most:} i
          +ts answers are perfect by construction, so stand-in rows measu
          +re delivery, not answer quality; and its slowdown under load i
          +s a guess I never observed, because free-tier limits kept call
          +s in flight under 16, so it never triggered.

       61  ## 4. Where the time goes
       62  
       63 -dc-04 on Groq took 11,395 ms, all before the first token. Wait
          -ing 9,057 ms, working 1,712 ms (understand 388, second draft 8
          -63, check 461), retrying 560 ms (a first draft that said "a ga
          -in of 6.4 points", computed and not in the paper, so the numbe
          -r check rejected it). The other 66 ms is retrieval and overhea
          -d. The redraft had to wait in the governor because the first d
          -raft's reservation had used the per-minute token budget. I kno
          -w this from the per-attempt trace each reply carries: step, mo
          -del, attempt, `queued_ms`, `duration_ms`, outcome, tokens (`re
          -sults/20261111-1657-eval-300w-k5-hybrid/replies.jsonl`). A dif
          -ferent slow one, mos-03 (7,840 ms), spent zero time waiting: G
          -roq took 3,258 ms to return 49 tokens on the routing call, aga
          -inst 537 ms usually.                                          
       63 +dc-04 on Groq: 11,395 ms, all before the first token. **Waitin
          +g** 9,057 ms, **working** 1,712 ms (understand 388, draft 863,
          + check 461), **retrying** 560 ms (a first draft that wrote "a 
          +gain of 6.4 points", computed, not in the paper, so the number
          + check rejected it), 66 ms retrieval and overhead. The redraft
          + waited because the first draft's reservation had spent the mi
          +nute's token budget. I know this from the per-attempt trace ev
          +ery reply carries (step, model, attempt, queued and working ms
          +, outcome, tokens). By contrast mos-03 (7,840 ms) never waited
          +: Groq took 3,258 ms for a 49-token routing reply that usually
          + takes 537.

       65  ## 5. When a limit is hit
       66  
       67 -**Per minute:** the governor holds the request until tokens re
          -fill, but only if that still leaves 3.1 s for draft and check 
          -before the 15 s deadline. Otherwise it drafts on the lean mode
          -l with three pieces (24 lean drafts across 143 requests under 
          -overload, 17 across 71 with halved limits). Otherwise the pers
          -on gets the top passages, quoted and marked unchecked, with "t
          -ry again in a minute". So everyone is delayed up to about 13 s
          -, some get a shorter answer from a smaller model, and nobody i
          -s dropped silently. In the burst, 20 of 50 got passages instea
          -d of an answer, and none took longer than 14.6 s. **Per day:**
          - the budget is checked before any model call. If the draft or 
          -check model has nothing left, the reply comes back in about 6 
          -ms. If only the router's model is out, routing is skipped and 
          -the score filter does its job.                                
       67 +**Per minute:** the governor holds a request until tokens refi
          +ll, if that still leaves 3.1 s for draft and check inside 15 s
          +. If not, the lean model drafts from three pieces (24 lean dra
          +fts over 143 requests in overload). If not even that, the pers
          +on gets the top passages marked unchecked and "try again in a 
          +minute". So everyone is delayed up to about 13 s, some get a s
          +maller model's answer, nobody is dropped silently. In the burs
          +t 20 of 50 got passages and none waited past 14.6 s. **Per day
          +:** the budget is checked before any model call; with no draft
          + or check budget left the reply comes in about 6 ms. If only t
          +he router's model is out, routing is skipped.

       87  ## 6. Retries
       88  
       90 -One retry budget of two per request, shared across steps, so a
          - slow request cannot become a retry storm. A 429 waits the exa
          -ct time in the body, never the rounded `retry-after`. A per-da
          -y 429 is never retried. A 5xx backs off 250 ms × 2^n with jitt
          -er. A timeout is retried only if a whole step still fits befor
          -e the deadline. **Retrying made it worse** in dc-04 above: the
          - redraft was right, but it needed a second full reservation an
          -d waited 9 s, turning a 2 s answer into 11 s.                 
       89 +Two retries per request, shared across steps, so one slow requ
          +est cannot become a storm. A 429 waits the exact time in the b
          +ody, not the rounded `retry-after`; a per-day 429 is never ret
          +ried; a 5xx backs off 250 ms × 2^n with jitter; a timeout is r
          +etried only if a whole step still fits. **It made things worse
          +** in dc-04: the redraft was right but needed a second full re
          +servation, waited 9 s, and turned a 2 s answer into 11 s.

       95  ## 8. What I'm unsure about, what I left out, what I learned
       96  
       98 -**Least certain:** the tokens-per-day limit. I never hit it. I
          -f it is a hard 200K per model per day, the check model (about 
          -2,700 tokens per answer) caps the system near 75 answers a day
          -, and my "try again in about 2 minutes" would be badly wrong. 
          -**Not built on purpose:** a cache for repeated questions (burs
          -ts after an outage are mostly the same question, but I wanted 
          -the numbers to show the uncached system), and streaming the dr
          -aft before the check. **Learned:** a token-per-minute limit ch
          -arged on `max_tokens` turns `max_tokens` into a throughput kno
          -b. My drafts use about 120 of the 400 I reserve, so about 70% 
          -of what I reserve is never used, and that is what forces the l
          -ean path for two-part questions.                              
       97 +**Least certain:** tokens per day. If it is a hard 200K per mo
          +del, the check model (about 2,700 tokens an answer) caps us ne
          +ar 75 answers a day and "about 2 minutes" is badly wrong. **Le
          +ft out on purpose:** a cache for repeated questions, since bur
          +sts after an outage repeat themselves but I wanted the uncache
          +d numbers; and streaming the draft before the check. **Learned
          +:** when tokens per minute are charged on `max_tokens`, `max_t
          +okens` is a throughput knob. My drafts use about 120 of the 40
          +0 I reserve, and that unused 70% is what pushes two-part quest
          +ions onto the lean path.

      18 -A Python asyncio pipeline with five steps. \textbf{Understand} 
         -(gpt-oss-20b) decides if the question is about the three papers
         - and splits two-part questions into paper-scoped searches. \tex
         -tbf{Retrieve} is a local BM25 tool over 300-word pieces tagged 
         -with paper, section and page. \textbf{Decide} refuses without a
         -ny model call when the best BM25 score is under 7.0. \textbf{Dr
         -aft} (gpt-oss-120b) answers only from the passages and cites pa
         -ssage ids. \textbf{Check} runs two gates: a deterministic one (
         -every cited id was retrieved, every number in the answer appear
         -s in its cited passage) and a model one (qwen3.8-27b, a differe
         -nt family, confirms support). A failed check gets one redraft w
         -ith specific feedback, then a refusal. A per-model budget gover
         -nor mirrors Groq's buckets, every request has a 15 s deadline, 
         -and under pressure the pipeline steps down: lean draft on gpt-o
         -ss-20b with three pieces, then the best passages quoted and lab
         -elled unchecked, then an honest budget message. A wrong answer 
         -is never on that ladder.                                       
      18 +A Python asyncio pipeline. \textbf{Understand} (gpt-oss-20b) ch

         +hecked, then a budget message. A wrong answer is never a step o

      39 -\textbf{The stand-in} is a Starlette server speaking the same s
         -treaming API, so switching is a base URL change. Each model has
         - per-minute and per-day buckets that refill continuously, charg
         -e prompt plus \texttt{max\_tokens}, and refuse with Groq-shaped
         - 429s. Time to first token is log-normal per model, fitted to t
         -he p50 and p95 of 20 real draft calls each (gpt-oss-120b 481 ms
         -, sigma 0.36). It can fail, succeed slowly, slow down above 16 
         -calls in flight, and stream a few words at a time. For gold que
         -stions it answers from the gold evidence, but only if retrieval
         - sent that passage. \textbf{Where it differs most:} its answers
         - are perfect by construction, so stand-in rows measure delivery
         -, not answer quality; and its slowdown under load is a guess I 
         -never observed, because free-tier limits kept calls in flight u
         -nder 16, so it never triggered.

  Results
  - Load shapes: all five ran against the stand-in at the free-tier limits we
    measured. Laptop sleep invalidated earlier attempts twice; those runs are
    kept and marked invalid.
  - Table 3: three retrieval settings, 20 fixed questions each, on real Groq.
    They score 75%, 75% and 80% correct, one question apart. Since dc-04 flips
    between runs on the same passages, 20 questions can't separate them, and the
    write-up says so. The hybrid setting first refused everything because the
    refusal threshold was on the BM25 scale; I fixed that and re-ran it.
  - Real-model quality on all 36 gold questions, current code:
    - 21 of 25 answerable questions correct and cited, 1 wrong (dc-07).
    - Every question the papers don't answer and every off-topic question was
      refused.
  - Under load: zero wrong answers in any shape. When a limit is near, requests
    wait in the queue (up to 13.7 s) instead of retrying.
  - Number you couldn't guess: the check step is 77 to 79% of the cost at Groq's
    list prices.
